People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve gotten sort of stuck in these little routines, and I know they sound small when I say them out loud, but they’re starting to interfere. Like, if I handle my wrist brace, I’ll wash my hands again afterward, even if I’ve already washed them and nothing is really dirty. And before I leave the house, I’ll check the stove, and then the front door, and then sometimes I go back and check them again.

There’s also this counting I do silently in my head, sort of going through steps before I can leave. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it slows me down. I’ll know, rationally, that I’ve done enough, but I still feel pulled to repeat it. And lately it’s been making me late, which is embarrassing at my age, honestly. I just… I don’t want these little habits taking over more than they already have.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. It’s not like I sit there and decide to think these things. They just sort of barge in, usually when I’m doing something ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift. I’ll suddenly get this ugly little thought that maybe I left something unsafe, or I touched something unclean, or somehow I’ve put someone at risk.

And the frustrating part is that I know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s probably completely out of proportion. I can tell myself, “You didn’t do anything dangerous,” or “That’s not actually dirty,” but the thought doesn’t leave politely just because I’ve reasoned with it. It sort of sits there and nags at me, and then I feel I have to do something to settle it. It’s embarrassing to admit, because it sounds so excessive when I say it out loud, but yes, they do feel intrusive.

They feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense—not from outside, not like a voice or anything like that. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?

And I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… to some extent. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life or anything as dramatic as that. I’m still managing on my own, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

But the routines before leaving the house have definitely interfered. There have been mornings when I’m trying to get out to the museum for a docent shift, and I get caught up checking or making sure things feel settled, and then suddenly I’m late. Not terribly late every time, but late enough that I feel embarrassed and flustered when I arrive.

And I have skipped a few museum events, too—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because getting out the door started to feel like too much effort. It’s as if the whole process becomes loaded, and I think, “Oh, never mind, it’s easier just not to go.” I don’t like admitting that, because the museum is important to me, and I don’t want these habits deciding what I do. But yes, they have made me avoid a few things.

It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it slows me down. I’ll know, rationally, that I’ve done enough, but I still feel pulled to repeat it. And lately it’s been making me late, which is embarrassing at my age, honestly. I just… I don’t want these little habits taking over more than they already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that disturb you or feel intrusive?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not like I sit there and decide to think these things. They just sort of barge in, usually when I’m doing something ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift. I’ll suddenly get this ugly little thought that maybe I left something unsafe, or I touched something unclean, or somehow I’ve put someone at risk.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s probably completely out of proportion. I can tell myself, “You didn’t do anything dangerous,” or “That’s not actually dirty,” but the thought doesn’t leave politely just because I’ve reasoned with it. It sort of sits there and nags at me, and then I feel I have to do something to settle it. It’s embarrassing to admit, because it sounds so excessive when I say it out loud, but yes, they do feel intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like tension or restlessness, when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very specific in my body, to be honest. I mean, I can tell I’m uneasy when the thought is there, but whether that’s tension or restlessness in a clear physical sense… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nIt’s more that the thought feels bothersome and I want it resolved. I might feel keyed up, I suppose, but I couldn’t say, “Oh, my shoulders tighten,” or “I feel it in my chest,” not with any confidence. I’m sorry, that’s probably not very helpful, but I haven’t really sorted out the physical side of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense—not from outside, not like a voice or anything like that. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations, places, or activities because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… to some extent. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life or anything as dramatic as that. I’m still managing on my own, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut the routines before leaving the house have definitely interfered. There have been mornings when I’m trying to get out to the museum for a docent shift, and I get caught up checking or making sure things feel settled, and then suddenly I’m late. Not terribly late every time, but late enough that I feel embarrassed and flustered when I arrive.\n\nAnd I have skipped a few museum events, too—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because getting out the door started to feel like too much effort. It’s as if the whole process becomes loaded, and I think, “Oh, never mind, it’s easier just not to go.” I don’t like admitting that, because the museum is important to me, and I don’t want these habits deciding what I do. But yes, they have made me avoid a few things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "Determine the level of distress caused by the intrusive thoughts", "question": "How much distress do these intrusive thoughts typically cause you?"}

They’re distressing, yes, but I’d say not overwhelmingly so most of the time. It’s more like… an ugly little thought barges in and won’t let me simply go on with what I was doing. I’ll be reading at home, for example, and suddenly I’m thinking, did I leave something unsafe? Or I’ll be getting ready to go to a museum shift and it’s, what if I touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk?

And I do know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s out of proportion. I can say to myself, “This is not reasonable, you don’t need to be this concerned.” But it still creates this nagging distress, this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve dealt with it somehow.

So… mild to moderate, maybe? It bothers me and interrupts me, definitely. But I’m not in a complete panic every time. It’s more persistent and wearing than dramatic.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose that’s exactly what it feels like, even though part of me is embarrassed to put it that plainly. It isn’t that I truly believe, in a clear-headed way, that if I don’t check the stove one more time the house will definitely burn down, or if I don’t wash my hands again after adjusting my wrist brace something terrible will happen. I know I’ve already done enough.

But in the moment, there’s this pressure—almost like I can’t quite move on until I’ve completed the little routine. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. Or I’ll check the stove and the front door before leaving, and then I’m halfway through getting ready and I think, did I really check it properly? So I go back and look again.

And the counting is similar. Before I leave, I sometimes silently count through steps in my head, like I’m making sure everything has been accounted for. It gives me a bit of relief, I suppose, or at least it quiets that anxious feeling for the moment. But then it also makes me late, which is maddening, because I can see what’s happening and still feel like I have to finish it before I can walk out the door.

It isn’t that I truly believe, in a clear-headed way, that if I don’t check the stove one more time the house will definitely burn down, or if I don’t wash my hands again after adjusting my wrist brace something terrible will happen. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. It gives me a bit of relief, I suppose, or at least it quiets that anxious feeling for the moment. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s a bit of both, I think, though if I’m being honest, it’s often less specific than it probably sounds. With the stove and the front door, there is a kind of specific worry underneath it—like, what if I left something on, what if the door isn’t really secure, what if something happens because I was careless. I don’t necessarily picture some detailed disaster every time, but there’s that sense of needing to make sure.

With the counting before I leave, that’s harder to explain. That’s more like trying to get a feeling that everything has been gone through properly. I’ll silently count through steps in my head, almost like I’m checking off that I’ve done what I’m supposed to do, and if it doesn’t feel complete, I get stuck. It’s not always that I think, “If I don’t count this way, this exact bad thing will happen.” It’s more that I feel unsettled and can’t quite leave.

And with the handwashing after handling my wrist brace, again, I know I’ve already done enough much of the time. But I’ll handle it, or adjust it, and then I feel like I need to wash again just to feel right about it. So yes, sometimes it’s tied to preventing something, and sometimes it’s just trying to quiet that anxious, unfinished feeling. The problem is that all these little routines add up, and then I’m late even though I knew, really, that I’d already checked or washed enough.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve gotten sort of stuck in these little routines, and I know they sound small when I say them out loud, but they’re starting to interfere. Like, if I handle my wrist brace, I’ll wash my hands again afterward, even if I’ve already washed them and nothing is really dirty. And before I leave the house, I’ll check the stove, and then the front door, and then sometimes I go back and check them again.\n\nThere’s also this counting I do silently in my head, sort of going through steps before I can leave. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it slows me down. I’ll know, rationally, that I’ve done enough, but I still feel pulled to repeat it. And lately it’s been making me late, which is embarrassing at my age, honestly. I just… I don’t want these little habits taking over more than they already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that disturb you or feel intrusive?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not like I sit there and decide to think these things. They just sort of barge in, usually when I’m doing something ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift. I’ll suddenly get this ugly little thought that maybe I left something unsafe, or I touched something unclean, or somehow I’ve put someone at risk.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s probably completely out of proportion. I can tell myself, “You didn’t do anything dangerous,” or “That’s not actually dirty,” but the thought doesn’t leave politely just because I’ve reasoned with it. It sort of sits there and nags at me, and then I feel I have to do something to settle it. It’s embarrassing to admit, because it sounds so excessive when I say it out loud, but yes, they do feel intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like tension or restlessness, when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very specific in my body, to be honest. I mean, I can tell I’m uneasy when the thought is there, but whether that’s tension or restlessness in a clear physical sense… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nIt’s more that the thought feels bothersome and I want it resolved. I might feel keyed up, I suppose, but I couldn’t say, “Oh, my shoulders tighten,” or “I feel it in my chest,” not with any confidence. I’m sorry, that’s probably not very helpful, but I haven’t really sorted out the physical side of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense—not from outside, not like a voice or anything like that. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations, places, or activities because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… to some extent. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations, places, or activities because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… to some extent. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life or anything as dramatic as that. I’m still managing on my own, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut the routines before leaving the house have definitely interfered. There have been mornings when I’m trying to get out to the museum for a docent shift, and I get caught up checking or making sure things feel settled, and then suddenly I’m late. Not terribly late every time, but late enough that I feel embarrassed and flustered when I arrive.\n\nAnd I have skipped a few museum events, too—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because getting out the door started to feel like too much effort. It’s as if the whole process becomes loaded, and I think, “Oh, never mind, it’s easier just not to go.” I don’t like admitting that, because the museum is important to me, and I don’t want these habits deciding what I do. But yes, they have made me avoid a few things.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these intrusive thoughts typically cause you?\n\nPatient: They’re distressing, yes, but I’d say not overwhelmingly so most of the time. It’s more like… an ugly little thought barges in and won’t let me simply go on with what I was doing. I’ll be reading at home, for example, and suddenly I’m thinking, did I leave something unsafe? Or I’ll be getting ready to go to a museum shift and it’s, what if I touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I do know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s out of proportion. I can say to myself, “This is not reasonable, you don’t need to be this concerned.” But it still creates this nagging distress, this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve dealt with it somehow.\n\nSo… mild to moderate, maybe? It bothers me and interrupts me, definitely. But I’m not in a complete panic every time. It’s more persistent and wearing than dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these rituals, like checking the stove or counting, in order to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s exactly what it feels like, even though part of me is embarrassed to put it that plainly. It isn’t that I truly believe, in a clear-headed way, that if I don’t check the stove one more time the house will definitely burn down, or if I don’t wash my hands again after adjusting my wrist brace something terrible will happen. I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nBut in the moment, there’s this pressure—almost like I can’t quite move on until I’ve completed the little routine. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. Or I’ll check the stove and the front door before leaving, and then I’m halfway through getting ready and I think, did I really check it properly? So I go back and look again.\n\nAnd the counting is similar. Before I leave, I sometimes silently count through steps in my head, like I’m making sure everything has been accounted for. It gives me a bit of relief, I suppose, or at least it quiets that anxious feeling for the moment. But then it also makes me late, which is maddening, because I can see what’s happening and still feel like I have to finish it before I can walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these rituals, such as checking the stove or counting, are you trying to prevent something specific from happening or are you just trying to feel less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think, though if I’m being honest, it’s often less specific than it probably sounds. With the stove and the front door, there is a kind of specific worry underneath it—like, what if I left something on, what if the door isn’t really secure, what if something happens because I was careless. I don’t necessarily picture some detailed disaster every time, but there’s that sense of needing to make sure.\n\nWith the counting before I leave, that’s harder to explain. That’s more like trying to get a feeling that everything has been gone through properly. I’ll silently count through steps in my head, almost like I’m checking off that I’ve done what I’m supposed to do, and if it doesn’t feel complete, I get stuck. It’s not always that I think, “If I don’t count this way, this exact bad thing will happen.” It’s more that I feel unsettled and can’t quite leave.\n\nAnd with the handwashing after handling my wrist brace, again, I know I’ve already done enough much of the time. But I’ll handle it, or adjust it, and then I feel like I need to wash again just to feel right about it. So yes, sometimes it’s tied to preventing something, and sometimes it’s just trying to quiet that anxious, unfinished feeling. The problem is that all these little routines add up, and then I’m late even though I knew, really, that I’d already checked or washed enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… though I’d say it’s not completely derailed my life or anything like that. I’m still managing on my own. I live independently, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I’m not working now, so it’s not as though I’m missing a job every morning.

But it has affected my docent work at the museum. That’s probably where it shows up most clearly. I’ll be trying to get out the door for a shift, and the checking and rechecking can eat up more time than I expect. I’ll think, “All right, just one more look,” and then somehow I’m still not settled, and then I’m late. Not terribly late every single time, but enough that I notice it and feel embarrassed walking in.

And I’ve skipped a few museum events too, which bothers me because I do enjoy them. It’s not that I didn’t want to go. It’s more that the process of getting out the door started to feel like too much—too many steps, too much uncertainty, too much of that feeling that I hadn’t done everything properly. So I’ve talked myself out of going, which is a shame.

As for relationships, I wouldn’t say it’s caused some dramatic falling-out with anyone. But it does make me less available sometimes, I suppose, because if I’m late or I cancel, people may not fully understand why. I tend not to explain all the details because it feels a bit foolish when I hear myself say it out loud.

I don’t necessarily picture some detailed disaster every time, but there’s that sense of needing to make sure.\n\nWith the counting before I leave, that’s harder to explain. That’s more like trying to get a feeling that everything has been gone through properly. I’ll silently count through steps in my head, almost like I’m checking off that I’ve done what I’m supposed to do, and if it doesn’t feel complete, I get stuck. It’s not always that I think, “If I don’t count this way, this exact bad thing will happen.” It’s more that I feel unsettled and can’t quite leave.\n\nAnd with the handwashing after handling my wrist brace, again, I know I’ve already done enough much of the time. But I’ll handle it, or adjust it, and then I feel like I need to wash again just to feel right about it. So yes, sometimes it’s tied to preventing something, and sometimes it’s just trying to quiet that anxious, unfinished feeling. The problem is that all these little routines add up, and then I’m late even though I knew, really, that I’d already checked or washed enough.\n\nDoctor: Have these habits and intrusive thoughts significantly impacted your ability to work or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I’d say it’s not completely derailed my life or anything like that. I’m still managing on my own. I live independently, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I’m not working now, so it’s not as though I’m missing a job every morning.\n\nBut it has affected my docent work at the museum. That’s probably where it shows up most clearly. I’ll be trying to get out the door for a shift, and the checking and rechecking can eat up more time than I expect. I’ll think, “All right, just one more look,” and then somehow I’m still not settled, and then I’m late. Not terribly late every single time, but enough that I notice it and feel embarrassed walking in.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few museum events too, which bothers me because I do enjoy them. It’s not that I didn’t want to go. It’s more that the process of getting out the door started to feel like too much—too many steps, too much uncertainty, too much of that feeling that I hadn’t done everything properly. So I’ve talked myself out of going, which is a shame.\n\nAs for relationships, I wouldn’t say it’s caused some dramatic falling-out with anyone. But it does make me less available sometimes, I suppose, because if I’m late or I cancel, people may not fully understand why. I tend not to explain all the details because it feels a bit foolish when I hear myself say it out loud.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’ll suddenly get this ugly little thought that maybe I left something unsafe, or I touched something unclean, or somehow I’ve put someone at risk.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s probably completely out of proportion. I can tell myself, “You didn’t do anything dangerous,” or “That’s not actually dirty,” but the thought doesn’t leave politely just because I’ve reasoned with it. It sort of sits there and nags at me, and then I feel I have to do something to settle it. It’s embarrassing to admit, because it sounds so excessive when I say it out loud, but yes, they do feel intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like tension or restlessness, when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very specific in my body, to be honest. I mean, I can tell I’m uneasy when the thought is there, but whether that’s tension or restlessness in a clear physical sense… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nIt’s more that the thought feels bothersome and I want it resolved. I might feel keyed up, I suppose, but I couldn’t say, “Oh, my shoulders tighten,” or “I feel it in my chest,” not with any confidence. I’m sorry, that’s probably not very helpful, but I haven’t really sorted out the physical side of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense—not from outside, not like a voice or anything like that. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations, places, or activities because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… to some extent. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life or anything as dramatic as that. I’m still managing on my own, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut the routines before leaving the house have definitely interfered. There have been mornings when I’m trying to get out to the museum for a docent shift, and I get caught up checking or making sure things feel settled, and then suddenly I’m late. Not terribly late every time, but late enough that I feel embarrassed and flustered when I arrive.\n\nAnd I have skipped a few museum events, too—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because getting out the door started to feel like too much effort. It’s as if the whole process becomes loaded, and I think, “Oh, never mind, it’s easier just not to go.” I don’t like admitting that, because the museum is important to me, and I don’t want these habits deciding what I do. But yes, they have made me avoid a few things.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these intrusive thoughts typically cause you?\n\nPatient: They’re distressing, yes, but I’d say not overwhelmingly so most of the time. It’s more like… an ugly little thought barges in and won’t let me simply go on with what I was doing. I’ll be reading at home, for example, and suddenly I’m thinking, did I leave something unsafe? Or I’ll be getting ready to go to a museum shift and it’s, what if I touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I do know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s out of proportion. I can say to myself, “This is not reasonable, you don’t need to be this concerned.” But it still creates this nagging distress, this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve dealt with it somehow.\n\nSo… mild to moderate, maybe? It bothers me and interrupts me, definitely. But I’m not in a complete panic every time. It’s more persistent and wearing than dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these rituals, like checking the stove or counting, in order to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s exactly what it feels like, even though part of me is embarrassed to put it that plainly. It isn’t that I truly believe, in a clear-headed way, that if I don’t check the stove one more time the house will definitely burn down, or if I don’t wash my hands again after adjusting my wrist brace something terrible will happen. I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nBut in the moment, there’s this pressure—almost like I can’t quite move on until I’ve completed the little routine. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
With family or friends, not in a major dramatic way, no. I’m still managing on my own, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve become completely unreliable or anything like that.

Where it shows up more is around the museum. I’ve been late to docent shifts because getting out the door turns into this whole drawn-out process, and by the time I’ve finished making sure everything feels settled, I’ve lost more time than I meant to. And I have skipped a few museum events altogether, not because I didn’t want to go, but because the thought of going through all those steps just felt like too much that day.

So I suppose it affects relationships indirectly. If I’m late, or if I don’t show up to something, people may not understand why, and I feel embarrassed about that. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused a serious rift with family or friends. It’s more that it makes me less present or dependable than I’d like to be.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve gotten sort of stuck in these little routines, and I know they sound small when I say them out loud, but they’re starting to interfere. Like, if I handle my wrist brace, I’ll wash my hands again afterward, even if I’ve already washed them and nothing is really dirty. And before I leave the house, I’ll check the stove, and then the front door, and then sometimes I go back and check them again.\n\nThere’s also this counting I do silently in my head, sort of going through steps before I can leave. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it slows me down. I’ll know, rationally, that I’ve done enough, but I still feel pulled to repeat it. And lately it’s been making me late, which is embarrassing at my age, honestly. I just… I don’t want these little habits taking over more than they already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that disturb you or feel intrusive?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not like I sit there and decide to think these things. They just sort of barge in, usually when I’m doing something ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift. I’ll suddenly get this ugly little thought that maybe I left something unsafe, or I touched something unclean, or somehow I’ve put someone at risk.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s probably completely out of proportion. I can tell myself, “You didn’t do anything dangerous,” or “That’s not actually dirty,” but the thought doesn’t leave politely just because I’ve reasoned with it. It sort of sits there and nags at me, and then I feel I have to do something to settle it. It’s embarrassing to admit, because it sounds so excessive when I say it out loud, but yes, they do feel intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like tension or restlessness, when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very specific in my body, to be honest. I mean, I can tell I’m uneasy when the thought is there, but whether that’s tension or restlessness in a clear physical sense… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nIt’s more that the thought feels bothersome and I want it resolved. I might feel keyed up, I suppose, but I couldn’t say, “Oh, my shoulders tighten,” or “I feel it in my chest,” not with any confidence. I’m sorry, that’s probably not very helpful, but I haven’t really sorted out the physical side of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense—not from outside, not like a voice or anything like that. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations, places, or activities because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… to some extent. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life or anything as dramatic as that. I’m still managing on my own, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut the routines before leaving the house have definitely interfered. There have been mornings when I’m trying to get out to the museum for a docent shift, and I get caught up checking or making sure things feel settled, and then suddenly I’m late. Not terribly late every time, but late enough that I feel embarrassed and flustered when I arrive.\n\nAnd I have skipped a few museum events, too—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because getting out the door started to feel like too much effort. It’s as if the whole process becomes loaded, and I think, “Oh, never mind, it’s easier just not to go.” I don’t like admitting that, because the museum is important to me, and I don’t want these habits deciding what I do. But yes, they have made me avoid a few things.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these intrusive thoughts typically cause you?\n\nPatient: They’re distressing, yes, but I’d say not overwhelmingly so most of the time. It’s more like… an ugly little thought barges in and won’t let me simply go on with what I was doing. I’ll be reading at home, for example, and suddenly I’m thinking, did I leave something unsafe? Or I’ll be getting ready to go to a museum shift and it’s, what if I touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I do know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s out of proportion. I can say to myself, “This is not reasonable, you don’t need to be this concerned.” But it still creates this nagging distress, this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve dealt with it somehow.\n\nSo… mild to moderate, maybe? It bothers me and interrupts me, definitely. But I’m not in a complete panic every time. It’s more persistent and wearing than dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these rituals, like checking the stove or counting, in order to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s exactly what it feels like, even though part of me is embarrassed to put it that plainly. It isn’t that I truly believe, in a clear-headed way, that if I don’t check the stove one more time the house will definitely burn down, or if I don’t wash my hands again after adjusting my wrist brace something terrible will happen. I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nBut in the moment, there’s this pressure—almost like I can’t quite move on until I’ve completed the little routine. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. Or I’ll check the stove and the front door before leaving, and then I’m halfway through getting ready and I think, did I really check it properly? So I go back and look again.\n\nAnd the counting is similar. Before I leave, I sometimes silently count through steps in my head, like I’m making sure everything has been accounted for. It gives me a bit of relief, I suppose, or at least it quiets that anxious feeling for the moment. But then it also makes me late, which is maddening, because I can see what’s happening and still feel like I have to finish it before I can walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these rituals, such as checking the stove or counting, are you trying to prevent something specific from happening or are you just trying to feel less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think, though if I’m being honest, it’s often less specific than it probably sounds. With the stove and the front door, there is a kind of specific worry underneath it—like, what if I left something on, what if the door isn’t really secure, what if something happens because I was careless. I don’t necessarily picture some detailed disaster every time, but there’s that sense of needing to make sure.\n\nWith the counting before I leave, that’s harder to explain. That’s more like trying to get a feeling that everything has been gone through properly. I’ll silently count through steps in my head, almost like I’m checking off that I’ve done what I’m supposed to do, and if it doesn’t feel complete, I get stuck. It’s not always that I think, “If I don’t count this way, this exact bad thing will happen.” It’s more that I feel unsettled and can’t quite leave.\n\nAnd with the handwashing after handling my wrist brace, again, I know I’ve already done enough much of the time. But I’ll handle it, or adjust it, and then I feel like I need to wash again just to feel right about it. So yes, sometimes it’s tied to preventing something, and sometimes it’s just trying to quiet that anxious, unfinished feeling. The problem is that all these little routines add up, and then I’m late even though I knew, really, that I’d already checked or washed enough.\n\nDoctor: Have these habits and intrusive thoughts significantly impacted your ability to work or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I’d say it’s not completely derailed my life or anything like that. I’m still managing on my own. I live independently, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I’m not working now, so it’s not as though I’m missing a job every morning.\n\nBut it has affected my docent work at the museum. That’s probably where it shows up most clearly. I’ll be trying to get out the door for a shift, and the checking and rechecking can eat up more time than I expect. I’ll think, “All right, just one more look,” and then somehow I’m still not settled, and then I’m late. Not terribly late every single time, but enough that I notice it and feel embarrassed walking in.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few museum events too, which bothers me because I do enjoy them. It’s not that I didn’t want to go. It’s more that the process of getting out the door started to feel like too much—too many steps, too much uncertainty, too much of that feeling that I hadn’t done everything properly. So I’ve talked myself out of going, which is a shame.\n\nAs for relationships, I wouldn’t say it’s caused some dramatic falling-out with anyone. But it does make me less available sometimes, I suppose, because if I’m late or I cancel, people may not fully understand why. I tend not to explain all the details because it feels a bit foolish when I hear myself say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends due to these habits and thoughts?\n\nPatient: With family or friends, not in a major dramatic way, no. I’m still managing on my own, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve become completely unreliable or anything like that.\n\nWhere it shows up more is around the museum. I’ve been late to docent shifts because getting out the door turns into this whole drawn-out process, and by the time I’ve finished making sure everything feels settled, I’ve lost more time than I meant to. And I have skipped a few museum events altogether, not because I didn’t want to go, but because the thought of going through all those steps just felt like too much that day.\n\nSo I suppose it affects relationships indirectly. If I’m late, or if I don’t show up to something, people may not understand why, and I feel embarrassed about that. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused a serious rift with family or friends. It’s more that it makes me less present or dependable than I’d like to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

If I’m late, or if I don’t show up to something, people may not understand why, and I feel embarrassed about that. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused a serious rift with family or friends. It’s more that it makes me less present or dependable than I’d like to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

I’m still managing on my own. That’s probably where it shows up most clearly. I’ll be trying to get out the door for a shift, and the checking and rechecking can eat up more time than I expect. I’m still managing on my own, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve become completely unreliable or anything like that.\n\nWhere it shows up more is around the museum. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly in a clear, direct way every time. With the stove or the front door, yes, there’s some of that—like I’m trying to make sure I haven’t left something unsafe or made some careless mistake. But most of the time it doesn’t feel as organized as, “I must do this or someone will be harmed.”

What brings me in is more that I get stuck in these little routines. I wash my hands again after handling my wrist brace, even when part of me knows I’ve already washed enough. I recheck the stove and the front door. I silently count through the steps before I leave, almost to make sure everything has been done properly. And if it doesn’t feel right or complete, I have trouble moving on.

So I suppose there is a fear of something being wrong underneath it, but a lot of it is just this nagging need to do it again until I feel settled. And that’s what’s started causing problems—because even when I know I’ve done enough, I keep repeating things, and then I end up late.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve gotten sort of stuck in these little routines, and I know they sound small when I say them out loud, but they’re starting to interfere. Like, if I handle my wrist brace, I’ll wash my hands again afterward, even if I’ve already washed them and nothing is really dirty. And before I leave the house, I’ll check the stove, and then the front door, and then sometimes I go back and check them again.\n\nThere’s also this counting I do silently in my head, sort of going through steps before I can leave. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it slows me down. I’ll know, rationally, that I’ve done enough, but I still feel pulled to repeat it. And lately it’s been making me late, which is embarrassing at my age, honestly. I just… I don’t want these little habits taking over more than they already have.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that disturb you or feel intrusive?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not like I sit there and decide to think these things. They just sort of barge in, usually when I’m doing something ordinary, like reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift. I’ll suddenly get this ugly little thought that maybe I left something unsafe, or I touched something unclean, or somehow I’ve put someone at risk.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s probably completely out of proportion. I can tell myself, “You didn’t do anything dangerous,” or “That’s not actually dirty,” but the thought doesn’t leave politely just because I’ve reasoned with it. It sort of sits there and nags at me, and then I feel I have to do something to settle it. It’s embarrassing to admit, because it sounds so excessive when I say it out loud, but yes, they do feel intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like tension or restlessness, when these intrusive thoughts occur?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very specific in my body, to be honest. I mean, I can tell I’m uneasy when the thought is there, but whether that’s tension or restlessness in a clear physical sense… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nIt’s more that the thought feels bothersome and I want it resolved. I might feel keyed up, I suppose, but I couldn’t say, “Oh, my shoulders tighten,” or “I feel it in my chest,” not with any confidence. I’m sorry, that’s probably not very helpful, but I haven’t really sorted out the physical side of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense—not from outside, not like a voice or anything like that. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations, places, or activities because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… to some extent. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life or anything as dramatic as that. I’m still managing on my own, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut the routines before leaving the house have definitely interfered. There have been mornings when I’m trying to get out to the museum for a docent shift, and I get caught up checking or making sure things feel settled, and then suddenly I’m late. Not terribly late every time, but late enough that I feel embarrassed and flustered when I arrive.\n\nAnd I have skipped a few museum events, too—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because getting out the door started to feel like too much effort. It’s as if the whole process becomes loaded, and I think, “Oh, never mind, it’s easier just not to go.” I don’t like admitting that, because the museum is important to me, and I don’t want these habits deciding what I do. But yes, they have made me avoid a few things.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these intrusive thoughts typically cause you?\n\nPatient: They’re distressing, yes, but I’d say not overwhelmingly so most of the time. It’s more like… an ugly little thought barges in and won’t let me simply go on with what I was doing. I’ll be reading at home, for example, and suddenly I’m thinking, did I leave something unsafe? Or I’ll be getting ready to go to a museum shift and it’s, what if I touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I do know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s out of proportion. I can say to myself, “This is not reasonable, you don’t need to be this concerned.” But it still creates this nagging distress, this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve dealt with it somehow.\n\nSo… mild to moderate, maybe? It bothers me and interrupts me, definitely. But I’m not in a complete panic every time. It’s more persistent and wearing than dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these rituals, like checking the stove or counting, in order to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s exactly what it feels like, even though part of me is embarrassed to put it that plainly. It isn’t that I truly believe, in a clear-headed way, that if I don’t check the stove one more time the house will definitely burn down, or if I don’t wash my hands again after adjusting my wrist brace something terrible will happen. I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nBut in the moment, there’s this pressure—almost like I can’t quite move on until I’ve completed the little routine. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. Or I’ll check the stove and the front door before leaving, and then I’m halfway through getting ready and I think, did I really check it properly? So I go back and look again.\n\nAnd the counting is similar. Before I leave, I sometimes silently count through steps in my head, like I’m making sure everything has been accounted for. It gives me a bit of relief, I suppose, or at least it quiets that anxious feeling for the moment. But then it also makes me late, which is maddening, because I can see what’s happening and still feel like I have to finish it before I can walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these rituals, such as checking the stove or counting, are you trying to prevent something specific from happening or are you just trying to feel less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think, though if I’m being honest, it’s often less specific than it probably sounds. With the stove and the front door, there is a kind of specific worry underneath it—like, what if I left something on, what if the door isn’t really secure, what if something happens because I was careless. I don’t necessarily picture some detailed disaster every time, but there’s that sense of needing to make sure.\n\nWith the counting before I leave, that’s harder to explain. That’s more like trying to get a feeling that everything has been gone through properly. I’ll silently count through steps in my head, almost like I’m checking off that I’ve done what I’m supposed to do, and if it doesn’t feel complete, I get stuck. It’s not always that I think, “If I don’t count this way, this exact bad thing will happen.” It’s more that I feel unsettled and can’t quite leave.\n\nAnd with the handwashing after handling my wrist brace, again, I know I’ve already done enough much of the time. But I’ll handle it, or adjust it, and then I feel like I need to wash again just to feel right about it. So yes, sometimes it’s tied to preventing something, and sometimes it’s just trying to quiet that anxious, unfinished feeling. The problem is that all these little routines add up, and then I’m late even though I knew, really, that I’d already checked or washed enough.\n\nDoctor: Have these habits and intrusive thoughts significantly impacted your ability to work or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I’d say it’s not completely derailed my life or anything like that. I’m still managing on my own. I live independently, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I’m not working now, so it’s not as though I’m missing a job every morning.\n\nBut it has affected my docent work at the museum. That’s probably where it shows up most clearly. I’ll be trying to get out the door for a shift, and the checking and rechecking can eat up more time than I expect. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. So I go back and look again.\n\nAnd the counting is similar. Before I leave, I sometimes silently count through steps in my head, like I’m making sure everything has been accounted for. It gives me a bit of relief, I suppose, or at least it quiets that anxious feeling for the moment. But then it also makes me late, which is maddening, because I can see what’s happening and still feel like I have to finish it before I can walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these rituals, such as checking the stove or counting, are you trying to prevent something specific from happening or are you just trying to feel less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think, though if I’m being honest, it’s often less specific than it probably sounds. With the stove and the front door, there is a kind of specific worry underneath it—like, what if I left something on, what if the door isn’t really secure, what if something happens because I was careless. I don’t necessarily picture some detailed disaster every time, but there’s that sense of needing to make sure.\n\nWith the counting before I leave, that’s harder to explain. That’s more like trying to get a feeling that everything has been gone through properly. I’ll silently count through steps in my head, almost like I’m checking off that I’ve done what I’m supposed to do, and if it doesn’t feel complete, I get stuck. It’s not always that I think, “If I don’t count this way, this exact bad thing will happen.” It’s more that I feel unsettled and can’t quite leave.\n\nAnd with the handwashing after handling my wrist brace, again, I know I’ve already done enough much of the time. But I’ll handle it, or adjust it, and then I feel like I need to wash again just to feel right about it. So yes, sometimes it’s tied to preventing something, and sometimes it’s just trying to quiet that anxious, unfinished feeling. The problem is that all these little routines add up, and then I’m late even though I knew, really, that I’d already checked or washed enough.\n\nDoctor: Have these habits and intrusive thoughts significantly impacted your ability to work or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I’d say it’s not completely derailed my life or anything like that. I’m still managing on my own. I live independently, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I’m not working now, so it’s not as though I’m missing a job every morning.\n\nBut it has affected my docent work at the museum. That’s probably where it shows up most clearly. I’ll be trying to get out the door for a shift, and the checking and rechecking can eat up more time than I expect. I’ll think, “All right, just one more look,” and then somehow I’m still not settled, and then I’m late. Not terribly late every single time, but enough that I notice it and feel embarrassed walking in.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few museum events too, which bothers me because I do enjoy them. It’s not that I didn’t want to go. It’s more that the process of getting out the door started to feel like too much—too many steps, too much uncertainty, too much of that feeling that I hadn’t done everything properly. So I’ve talked myself out of going, which is a shame.\n\nAs for relationships, I wouldn’t say it’s caused some dramatic falling-out with anyone. But it does make me less available sometimes, I suppose, because if I’m late or I cancel, people may not fully understand why. I tend not to explain all the details because it feels a bit foolish when I hear myself say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends due to these habits and thoughts?\n\nPatient: With family or friends, not in a major dramatic way, no. I’m still managing on my own, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve become completely unreliable or anything like that.\n\nWhere it shows up more is around the museum. I’ve been late to docent shifts because getting out the door turns into this whole drawn-out process, and by the time I’ve finished making sure everything feels settled, I’ve lost more time than I meant to. And I have skipped a few museum events altogether, not because I didn’t want to go, but because the thought of going through all those steps just felt like too much that day.\n\nSo I suppose it affects relationships indirectly. If I’m late, or if I don’t show up to something, people may not understand why, and I feel embarrassed about that. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused a serious rift with family or friends. It’s more that it makes me less present or dependable than I’d like to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a clear, direct way every time. With the stove or the front door, yes, there’s some of that—like I’m trying to make sure I haven’t left something unsafe or made some careless mistake. But most of the time it doesn’t feel as organized as, “I must do this or someone will be harmed.”\n\nWhat brings me in is more that I get stuck in these little routines. I wash my hands again after handling my wrist brace, even when part of me knows I’ve already washed enough. I recheck the stove and the front door. I silently count through the steps before I leave, almost to make sure everything has been done properly. And if it doesn’t feel right or complete, I have trouble moving on.\n\nSo I suppose there is a fear of something being wrong underneath it, but a lot of it is just this nagging need to do it again until I feel settled. And that’s what’s started causing problems—because even when I know I’ve done enough, I keep repeating things, and then I end up late.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think I may have said part of this already, but… no, not always in the sense of, “If I don’t do this, someone will definitely be hurt.” It’s more that I get caught in the routines themselves.

What brings me in, really, is that I’ve gotten stuck doing these little repeated things. I’ll wash my hands again after handling my wrist brace, even if I know perfectly well I washed them already. Or I’ll go back and check the stove, then the front door, and then sometimes I’m not quite satisfied and I’ll check again. Before I leave, I’ll silently count through the steps in my head, almost like I’m trying to make sure the whole sequence has been completed properly.

And the frustrating part is that I do often know I’ve done enough. I can even tell myself, “You’ve checked it, it’s fine, you can go now.” But it doesn’t feel finished, somehow. So I repeat it, and then suddenly I’ve lost time and I’m running late. So it’s not only about preventing harm, exactly—it’s more that I can’t seem to let the routine stop when it should.

And before I leave the house, I’ll check the stove, and then the front door, and then sometimes I go back and check them again.\n\nThere’s also this counting I do silently in my head, sort of going through steps before I can leave. It sort of sits there and nags at me, and then I feel I have to do something to settle it. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nIt’s more that the thought feels bothersome and I want it resolved. I might feel keyed up, I suppose, but I couldn’t say, “Oh, my shoulders tighten,” or “I feel it in my chest,” not with any confidence. I’m sorry, that’s probably not very helpful, but I haven’t really sorted out the physical side of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense—not from outside, not like a voice or anything like that. It’s my own mind producing them, which is part of what makes it so frustrating, because I’ll be sitting there reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this ugly little thought just barges in: What if I left something unsafe? What if I touched something unclean? What if I’ve somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I know perfectly well, or at least the reasonable part of me knows, that it’s out of proportion. It doesn’t feel like “someone else” is putting it there. It feels like my own thought, just one I don’t want and don’t agree with. That’s the odd thing—I can recognize it as mine and still feel almost ambushed by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations, places, or activities because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… to some extent. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped living my life or anything as dramatic as that. I’m still managing on my own, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut the routines before leaving the house have definitely interfered. There have been mornings when I’m trying to get out to the museum for a docent shift, and I get caught up checking or making sure things feel settled, and then suddenly I’m late. Not terribly late every time, but late enough that I feel embarrassed and flustered when I arrive.\n\nAnd I have skipped a few museum events, too—not because I didn’t want to go, exactly, but because getting out the door started to feel like too much effort. It’s as if the whole process becomes loaded, and I think, “Oh, never mind, it’s easier just not to go.” I don’t like admitting that, because the museum is important to me, and I don’t want these habits deciding what I do. But yes, they have made me avoid a few things.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these intrusive thoughts typically cause you?\n\nPatient: They’re distressing, yes, but I’d say not overwhelmingly so most of the time. It’s more like… an ugly little thought barges in and won’t let me simply go on with what I was doing. I’ll be reading at home, for example, and suddenly I’m thinking, did I leave something unsafe? Or I’ll be getting ready to go to a museum shift and it’s, what if I touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk?\n\nAnd I do know, in the sensible part of my mind, that it’s out of proportion. I can say to myself, “This is not reasonable, you don’t need to be this concerned.” But it still creates this nagging distress, this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve dealt with it somehow.\n\nSo… mild to moderate, maybe? It bothers me and interrupts me, definitely. But I’m not in a complete panic every time. It’s more persistent and wearing than dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these rituals, like checking the stove or counting, in order to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s exactly what it feels like, even though part of me is embarrassed to put it that plainly. It isn’t that I truly believe, in a clear-headed way, that if I don’t check the stove one more time the house will definitely burn down, or if I don’t wash my hands again after adjusting my wrist brace something terrible will happen. I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nBut in the moment, there’s this pressure—almost like I can’t quite move on until I’ve completed the little routine. I’ll wash my hands, then touch or handle the brace, and then I feel pulled to wash them again, even if I know there’s no real reason. Or I’ll check the stove and the front door before leaving, and then I’m halfway through getting ready and I think, did I really check it properly? So I go back and look again.\n\nAnd the counting is similar. Before I leave, I sometimes silently count through steps in my head, like I’m making sure everything has been accounted for. It gives me a bit of relief, I suppose, or at least it quiets that anxious feeling for the moment. But then it also makes me late, which is maddening, because I can see what’s happening and still feel like I have to finish it before I can walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these rituals, such as checking the stove or counting, are you trying to prevent something specific from happening or are you just trying to feel less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think, though if I’m being honest, it’s often less specific than it probably sounds. With the stove and the front door, there is a kind of specific worry underneath it—like, what if I left something on, what if the door isn’t really secure, what if something happens because I was careless. I don’t necessarily picture some detailed disaster every time, but there’s that sense of needing to make sure.\n\nWith the counting before I leave, that’s harder to explain. That’s more like trying to get a feeling that everything has been gone through properly. I’ll silently count through steps in my head, almost like I’m checking off that I’ve done what I’m supposed to do, and if it doesn’t feel complete, I get stuck. It’s not always that I think, “If I don’t count this way, this exact bad thing will happen.” It’s more that I feel unsettled and can’t quite leave.\n\nAnd with the handwashing after handling my wrist brace, again, I know I’ve already done enough much of the time. But I’ll handle it, or adjust it, and then I feel like I need to wash again just to feel right about it. So yes, sometimes it’s tied to preventing something, and sometimes it’s just trying to quiet that anxious, unfinished feeling. The problem is that all these little routines add up, and then I’m late even though I knew, really, that I’d already checked or washed enough.\n\nDoctor: Have these habits and intrusive thoughts significantly impacted your ability to work or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I’d say it’s not completely derailed my life or anything like that. I’m still managing on my own. I live independently, I get myself where I need to go most of the time, and I’m not working now, so it’s not as though I’m missing a job every morning.\n\nBut it has affected my docent work at the museum. That’s probably where it shows up most clearly. I’ll be trying to get out the door for a shift, and the checking and rechecking can eat up more time than I expect. I’ll think, “All right, just one more look,” and then somehow I’m still not settled, and then I’m late. Not terribly late every single time, but enough that I notice it and feel embarrassed walking in.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped a few museum events too, which bothers me because I do enjoy them. It’s not that I didn’t want to go. It’s more that the process of getting out the door started to feel like too much—too many steps, too much uncertainty, too much of that feeling that I hadn’t done everything properly. So I’ve talked myself out of going, which is a shame.\n\nAs for relationships, I wouldn’t say it’s caused some dramatic falling-out with anyone. But it does make me less available sometimes, I suppose, because if I’m late or I cancel, people may not fully understand why. I tend not to explain all the details because it feels a bit foolish when I hear myself say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family or friends due to these habits and thoughts?\n\nPatient: With family or friends, not in a major dramatic way, no. I’m still managing on my own, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve become completely unreliable or anything like that.\n\nWhere it shows up more is around the museum. I’ve been late to docent shifts because getting out the door turns into this whole drawn-out process, and by the time I’ve finished making sure everything feels settled, I’ve lost more time than I meant to. And I have skipped a few museum events altogether, not because I didn’t want to go, but because the thought of going through all those steps just felt like too much that day.\n\nSo I suppose it affects relationships indirectly. If I’m late, or if I don’t show up to something, people may not understand why, and I feel embarrassed about that. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused a serious rift with family or friends. It’s more that it makes me less present or dependable than I’d like to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a clear, direct way every time. With the stove or the front door, yes, there’s some of that—like I’m trying to make sure I haven’t left something unsafe or made some careless mistake. But most of the time it doesn’t feel as organized as, “I must do this or someone will be harmed.”\n\nWhat brings me in is more that I get stuck in these little routines. I wash my hands again after handling my wrist brace, even when part of me knows I’ve already washed enough. I recheck the stove and the front door. I silently count through the steps before I leave, almost to make sure everything has been done properly. And if it doesn’t feel right or complete, I have trouble moving on.\n\nSo I suppose there is a fear of something being wrong underneath it, but a lot of it is just this nagging need to do it again until I feel settled. And that’s what’s started causing problems—because even when I know I’ve done enough, I keep repeating things, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: I think I may have said part of this already, but… no, not always in the sense of, “If I don’t do this, someone will definitely be hurt.” It’s more that I get caught in the routines themselves.\n\nWhat brings me in, really, is that I’ve gotten stuck doing these little repeated things. I’ll wash my hands again after handling my wrist brace, even if I know perfectly well I washed them already. Or I’ll go back and check the stove, then the front door, and then sometimes I’m not quite satisfied and I’ll check again. Before I leave, I’ll silently count through the steps in my head, almost like I’m trying to make sure the whole sequence has been completed properly.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that I do often know I’ve done enough. I can even tell myself, “You’ve checked it, it’s fine, you can go now.” But it doesn’t feel finished, somehow. So I repeat it, and then suddenly I’ve lost time and I’m running late.
